Hydrodynamic limit of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system for gas mixture Thanks:
Yeping Li, Gaofeng Wang and Tianfang Wu

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the hydrodynamic limit of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system for a gas mixture in the whole space ( x ∈ ℝ 3 ) (x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}) with the potential range of γ ∈ ( − 3 , 1 ] \gamma\in\left(-3,1\right] . Using the method of Hilbert expansion, we first derive a bi-Maxwellian determined by the Euler-Poisson system of two fluids. To justify the convergence of the solution rigorously as the Knudsen number tends to zero, we sequentially calculate the first 2 ​ k − 1 2k-1 terms of the expansion series ( k ≥ 6 ) (k\geq 6) , and then truncate it, and express the solution as the sum of these first 2 ​ k − 1 2k-1 terms and a remainder term. Within the framework of the L x , v 2 − W x , v 1 , ∞ L_{x,v}^{2}-W_{x,v}^{1,\infty} interplay established by Guo and Jang [ 15 ] , we construct a new weight function to estimate the remainder term in four different cases regarding the potential γ \gamma . Here, the particle masses m A , m B > 0 m^{A},m^{B}>0 and their charges e A , e B e^{A},e^{B} can be given arbitrarily. This causes the collision operator to exhibit asymmetric effects ( m A ≠ m B m^{A}\neq m^{B} ), rendering the system of equations impossible to decouple. So, it adds difficulties to both L 2 L^{2} , L ∞ L^{\infty} estimates for the remainder. Therefore, we adopt the framework of vector-valued functions and analyze the velocity decay rate of the operator K M , 2 , w α , c K_{M,2,w}^{\alpha,c} to eliminate the singularity induced by small parameters in characteristic line iterations. Our results show that the validity time of the solution is O ⁡ ( ε − y ) O(\varepsilon^{-y}) , where y y is − 2 ​ k − 3 2 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) -\frac{2k-3}{2(2k-1)} when − 1 ≤ γ ≤ 1 -1\leq\gamma\leq 1 , and it becomes − 2 ​ k − 3 ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) -\frac{2k-3}{(1-\gamma)(2k-1)} , when − 3 < γ < − 1 -3<\gamma<-1 . These results possess strong physical realism and can be applied to analyze gas flow dynamics in the daytime ionosphere at high altitudes above the Earth. Keywords . Hydrodynamic limit; Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system; Gas mixture; Particle masses; Hilbert expansion; Asymmetric effects; Soft potential; Validity time; Ionosphere. AMS subject classifications: 35B38, 35J47



1.
Introduction and main result

1.1.
Introduction

This paper studies the hydrodynamic limit of the
Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann (denoted as VPB in the sequel) system for a
gas mixture containing two types of molecules with different
molecular masses. We work with the following non-dimensionalized
form of the VPB system, which describes the evolution of two species
of particles interacting under the influence of a self-consistent
electric field:

\left\{\begin{gathered}St\,\partial_{t}F^{A}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{A}+\frac{e^{A}}{m^{A}}\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{A}=\frac{1}{Kn}\Big[Q^{AA}(F^{A},F^{A})+Q^{AB}(F^{A},F^{B})\Big],\\
St\,\partial_{t}F^{B}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{B}+\frac{e_{B}}{m^{B}}\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{B}=\frac{1}{Kn}\Big[Q^{BA}(F^{B},F^{A})+Q^{BB}(F^{B},F^{B})\Big],\\
\Delta\phi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F^{A}+e^{B}F^{B}dv-\bar{n}_{e}.\end{gathered}\right.

(1.1)

Here, we denote particle species by the Greek letters

\alpha
and
\beta
(
\alpha,\beta\in\{A,B\}
). The density
distribution function for particle species
\alpha
is given by

F^{\alpha}(t,x,v)
, defined at time
t>0
, position

x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and velocity

v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Particle mass, charge and particle
diameter are denoted by
m^{\alpha}>0
,
e^{\alpha}
and

\sigma_{\alpha}>0
, respectively. The self-consistent electric
potential
\phi(t,x)
is coupled with the distribution function
F^{\alpha}(t,x,v)
through the
Poisson equation
\eqref{MAMAMAMAzhuyaotuimox}_{3}
. The constant ion background charge is denoted by
\bar{n}_{e}
(defined in (
2.7
)).
St
and
Kn
are two dimensionless parameters. More precisely, the Strouhal number
St
characterizes the frequency of oscillatory flow phenomena, such as vortex shedding, in relation to flow velocity and a characteristic length. The Knudsen number
Kn
is defined as the ratio of the gas molecular mean free path to a characteristic physical length scale. Interactions are modeled by the Boltzmann collision operator for

\alpha
-
\beta
particle pairs

Q^{\alpha\beta}(F^{\alpha},F^{\beta})=\frac{(\sigma_{\alpha}+\sigma_{\beta})^{2}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\theta)\Big[F^{\alpha}{{}^{\prime}}F_{*}^{\beta}{{}^{\prime}}-F^{\alpha}F_{*}^{\beta}\Big]d\omega dv_{*},

where
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
and we use the shorthands
F^{\alpha}=F^{\alpha}(v)
,
F_{*}^{\alpha^{\prime}}=F^{\alpha}(v^{\prime}_{*})
,
F^{\alpha^{\prime}}=F^{\alpha}(v^{\prime})
,
F^{\alpha}_{*}=F^{\alpha}(v_{*})
. The velocity pairs
(v_{*},v)
and
(v^{\prime}_{*},v^{\prime})
represent the pre- and post-collision states of particles belonging to species
\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively. They obey the laws of conservation of momentum and energy:

m^{\alpha}v_{*}^{\prime}+m^{\beta}v^{\prime}=m^{\alpha}v_{*}+m^{\beta}v,\quad m^{\alpha}|v_{*}^{\prime}|^{2}+m^{\beta}|v^{\prime}|^{2}=m^{\alpha}|v_{*}|^{2}+m^{\beta}|v|^{2}.

The velocity
v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{*}
after the collision are expressed by

\left\{\begin{gathered}v^{\prime}=v-\frac{2m^{\beta}}{m^{\alpha}+m^{\beta}}[(v-v_{*})\cdot\omega]\omega,\\
v^{\prime}_{*}=v_{*}+\frac{2m^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}+m^{\beta}}[(v-v_{*})\cdot\omega]\omega.\end{gathered}\right.

Throughout this paper, we denote the Knudsen number by
Kn=\varepsilon
, where

\varepsilon
is assumed to be small.
The collision kernels satisfy the following assumptions:

(1) The symmetry of collision kernels:

B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\theta)=B^{\beta\alpha}(|v-v_{*}|,\theta)\,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\{A,B\}.

(2) The collision kernels could be decomposed into

B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\theta)=\Phi^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|)b^{\alpha\beta}(\cos\theta)\,\quad\alpha,\beta\in\{A,B\},

•

The kinetic part has the following form

\Phi^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|)=C_{\alpha\beta}^{\Phi}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma},\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{\Phi}>0,\,\gamma\in(-3,1].

•

Angular part has a strong form of Grad’s angular cutoff
[
12
]
: For
\cos\theta=\frac{\omega\cdot(v-v_{*})}{|v-v_{*}|}
, there exist a constant
C_{b}>0
, for
\forall\alpha,\beta\in\{A,B\}
and
\theta\in[0,\pi]
, such that

0<b^{\alpha\beta}(\cos\theta)\leq C_{b}|\cos\theta|.

The rigorous derivation of hydrodynamic limits from kinetic theory is a fundamental challenge in mathematical physics, directly addressing Hilbert’s sixth problem
[
21
]
concerning the mathematical unification of microscopic and macroscopic descriptions. In the asymptotic limit where the Knudsen number
\varepsilon\to 0
, the system transitions from kinetic to hydrodynamic regimes, with characteristic macroscopic scales by far exceeding the molecular mean free path.

Maxwell
[
30
]
and Boltzmann

[
7
]
first showed that the limiting solution of the
Boltzmann equation approaches a local Maxwellian, and its density,
momentum, and temperature correspond to a fluid system. These
contributions laid the foundation for further investigations into
the Boltzmann equation. Justifying the hydrodynamic limits that
arise from the Boltzmann equations presents significant challenges
and has been a topic of research for over five decades.

Caflisch
[
9
]
validated the compressible Euler limit via truncated Hilbert expansion but imposed constraints on initial data. Guo, Jang, and Jiang
[
19
]
refined this using the
L_{x,v}^{2}-L_{x,v}^{\infty}
technique
[
17
]
, later extending to the acoustic limit
[
20
]
. Note that Hilbert expansion requires initial values to match its form, however, for general cases, initial layers are needed
[
28
]
. The VPB system’s various scalings yield different fluid models, e.g., incompressible Navier-Stokes-Fourier-Poisson
[
25
]
,
[
33
]
. For further details, see
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
, and
[
32
]
.
Boundary effects complicate Euler limits: Sone
[
34
]
demonstrated viscous/Knudsen layers’ necessity. Boundary treatments include: specular reflection in
[
18
]
, diffuse/Maxwell conditions in
[
23
]
and
[
24
]
.

While monatomic gases dominate hydrodynamic limit studies, multi-species Boltzmann systems receive less attention. Aoki, Bardos, and Takata
[
2
]
proved the existence of a Knudsen layer for Boltzmann equations in gas mixtures with hard sphere interactions (zero bulk velocity assumption), later extended by Bardos and Yang
[
5
]
to include drifting velocities. Wang
[
36
]
studied the VPB system’s diffusive limit, which decouples for equal molecular collision masses
(m^{A}=m^{B})
in periodic domains, and later analyzed its decay rate
[
37
]
. Fang and Qi
[
11
]
examined the hydrodynamic transition from Boltzmann equations for gas mixtures
(m^{A}=m^{B})
to two-fluid systems. Guo
[
14
]
proved the Euler-Maxwell limit for hard spheres, while Jiang, Lei, and Zhao
[
26
]
investigated this limit, (
m_{\pm}=e_{\pm}=1
). Duan and Liu
[
10
]
discussed the Euler-Poisson limit for 1D gas mixtures.

However, in the real world, collisions between gas molecules often occur between two particles with different molecular masses. For example, the mass ratio between oxygen molecules and nitrogen molecules in the atmosphere is 8 to 7.

When considering collisions involving charged particles, the study of ionized gas mixtures is of paramount importance in atmospheric physics, especially in the ionosphere situated 60 km above Earth’s surface. This atmospheric layer exhibits a highly ionized state due to intense ultraviolet radiation and cosmic ray bombardment. ‌For example‌, in the atmospheric E layer or F1 layer , some nitrogen and oxygen are converted into nitrosyl ions (NO
+
,
m_{NO^{+}}

\approx
30
m_{p}
,
q_{NO^{+}}

=+1e
) and oxygen ions (O
+
,
m_{O^{+}}

\approx
16
m_{p}
,
q_{O^{+}}

=+1e
) under strong solar radiation during the daytime, leading to collisions between these ion species. In addition, with increasing high-altitude radiation, the atmosphere exhibits ions of different species, with their primary distribution as follows
[
1
]
,
[
22
]
:

\bullet
D layer (60–90 km):
NO^{+}
,
O_{2^{+}}
,
H_{2}O^{+}
,
H_{3}O^{+}
,
NO_{2^{-}}
,
O_{2^{-}}
.

\bullet
E layer (90–140 km):
N_{2^{+}}
,
O_{2^{+}}
,
NO^{+}
,
O^{+}
.

\bullet
F layer = F1 layer + F2 layer (140–1000 km):

•

F1 layer (140–210 km):
O^{+}
,
NO^{+}
,
O_{2^{+}}
.

•

F2 layer (210–1000 km):
O^{+}
,
NO^{+}
,
He^{+}
,
H^{+}
,
N^{+}
.

The particle masses and mass-to-charge ratios of these ion species exhibit variations. The dominant ions in both the D and E layers are NO
+
, with concentration ranges of
10^{5}
–
10^{6}

cm
-3
for the D layer and
10^{6}
–
10^{7}

cm
-3
for the E layer. In contrast, O
+
is the primary ion in both the F1 and F2 layers, with concentrations ranging approximately from
10^{6}
–
10^{7}

cm
-3
.

Current research exhibits a notable gap in mathematically formalizing these collision dynamics within the Boltzmann equation framework, especially when the assumption of equal masses or the normalization of both mass and charge becomes invalid. Our work systematically examines scenarios involving arbitrary mass and charge configurations, thereby establishing a generalized theoretical framework for particle collision phenomena in the ionosphere. Additionally, we construct appropriate weightings that enable both soft and hard potentials to be addressed within a unified framework.

Figure 1.
The collision between the nitrosyl ion and the oxygen ion

This paper investigates the hydrodynamic limit of a VPB system for binary gas mixtures with:

★

Potential range:
\gamma\in(-3,1]
,

★

Non-identical molecular masses:
m^{A}\neq m^{B}
,

★

Arbitrary charge configurations:
e^{A},e^{B}\in\mathbb{R}
,

★

Characteristic timescale
O(1)
,
requiring Strouhal number
St

to be
O(1)
.

The inherent coupling of this system necessitates analysis within a vector-valued function framework. Building on the
L_{x,v}^{2}-W_{x,v}^{1,\infty}
technique developed by
[
15
]
, we establish remainder estimates through weighted function analysis across four regimes:

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=1\text{ (hard sphere)},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\gamma<1\text{ (hard potentials \& Maxwell molecules)},

\displaystyle-1

\displaystyle\leq\gamma<0\text{ (moderately soft potentials)},

\displaystyle-3

\displaystyle<\gamma<-1\text{ (very soft potentials)}.

Key technical innovations include:

(1)

Well-designed weight function (
1.14
)-(
1.16
).

(2)

The velocity decay rate analysis of the operator
K_{M,2,w}^{\alpha,c}
(
m^{A}\neq m^{B}
case, see Lemma
4.3
).

(3)

Resolution of the
(1+|v|)^{\gamma}
degeneracy as
|v|\to\infty
(soft potential case, see (
5.8
)).

(4)

Control of remainder support propagation (soft potential case, see (
5.10
),(
5.11
)).

According to the Hilbert expansion

F_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}F^{\alpha}_{i}+\varepsilon^{k}F_{R}^{\alpha},\qquad\nabla_{x}\phi_{\epsilon}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon^{k}\nabla_{x}\phi_{R},\quad k\geq 6,

our results also compare the effects of different potential parameters
\gamma
on the weight function within the same theoretical framework. The analysis reveals that the solution’s validity time scales as
O(\varepsilon^{-y})
(see Figure 2), where the exponent
y
is determined by:

y=\begin{cases}-\dfrac{2k-3}{2(2k-1)}&\text{for }-1\leq\gamma\leq 1,\\[10.0pt]
-\dfrac{2k-3}{(1-\gamma)(2k-1)}&\text{for }-3<\gamma<-1.\end{cases}

Figure 2.
The validity time of the solution is
O(\varepsilon^{-y})

1.2.
Basic properties of collision operator (see
[
8
]
)

Firstly, we present some properties of the collision operators for later use. For the vector field
\mathbf{F}=(F^{A},F^{B})^{T}
, the vector-form bilinear Boltzmann collision operator
\mathscr{C}
is defined as:

\mathscr{C}\mathbf{F}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}Q^{AA}(F^{A},F^{A})+Q^{AB}(F^{A},F^{B})\\
Q^{BA}(F^{B},F^{A})+Q^{BB}(F^{B},F^{B})\end{array}\right).

(1.2)

•

(Collision invariants) A function
\mathbf{\Psi}=(\Psi^{A},\Psi^{B})^{T}

is called a collision invariant of the operator
\mathscr{C}
with respect to the inner product in
(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}
,
if it satisfies

\langle\mathscr{C}\mathbf{F},\mathbf{\Psi}\rangle_{(L_{v}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in{A,B}}\left\langle Q^{\alpha\beta}(F^{\alpha},F^{\beta}),\Psi^{\alpha}\right\rangle_{L_{v}^{2}}=0\quad\text{for any vector }\mathbf{F}.

(1.3)

The equality
\langle\mathscr{C}\mathbf{F},\mathbf{\Psi}\rangle_{(L_{v}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}}=0

holds, if and only if

\mathbf{\Psi}\in\text{Span}\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2},v_{1}\mathbf{m},v_{2}\mathbf{m},v_{3}\mathbf{m},|v|^{2}\mathbf{m}\},

where
\mathbf{m}=(m^{A},m^{B})^{T}

and
\mathbf{e}_{j}

is the
j^{\text{th}}

unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

•

(H-theorem) The entropy function of the Boltzmann equations for binary gas mixtures satisfies

\left\langle\mathscr{C}\mathbf{F},\log\mathbf{F}\right\rangle_{(L_{v}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in\{A,B\}}\left\langle Q^{\alpha\beta}(F^{\alpha},F^{\beta}),~~\log F^{\alpha}\right\rangle_{L_{v}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq 0,

(1.4)

and this equality holds, if and only if

\log\mathbf{F}\in{\rm Span}\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2},v_{1}\mathbf{m},v_{2}\mathbf{m},v_{3}\mathbf{m},|v|^{2}\mathbf{m}\}.

(1.5)

1.3.
Hilbert Expansion

We take
St=1
to rewrite the VPB system (
1.1
):

\left\{\begin{gathered}\partial_{t}F_{\varepsilon}^{A}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{\varepsilon}^{A}+\frac{e^{A}}{m^{A}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\epsilon}^{A}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{AA}(F_{\varepsilon}^{A},F_{\epsilon}^{A})+Q^{AB}(F_{\varepsilon}^{A},F_{\varepsilon}^{B})\Big],\\
\partial_{t}F_{\varepsilon}^{B}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{\varepsilon}^{B}+\frac{e_{B}}{m^{B}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\varepsilon}^{B}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big[Q^{BA}(F_{\varepsilon}^{B},F_{\varepsilon}^{A})+Q^{BB}(F_{\varepsilon}^{B},F_{\epsilon}^{B})\Big],\\
\Delta\phi_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F_{\varepsilon}^{A}+e^{B}F_{\varepsilon}^{B}dv-\bar{n}_{e}.\end{gathered}\right.

(1.6)

We assume the solutions of (
1.6
) have the form of Hilbert expansion:

F_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}F^{\alpha}_{i}+\varepsilon^{k}F_{R}^{\alpha},\qquad\nabla_{x}\phi_{\epsilon}=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}+\varepsilon^{k}\nabla_{x}\phi_{R},\quad k\geq 6.

(1.7)

Substituting (
1.7
) into the system (
1.6
), and compare the coefficients about order of
\varepsilon
, one derives that

\displaystyle\text{Order ~$O(\varepsilon^{-1})$:}\qquad\quad~~\qquad\qquad~\,~~\,0=\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{0}^{\alpha},F_{0}^{\beta}),

(1.8)

\displaystyle\text{Order ~$O(1)$:}\qquad\quad~~~~(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{0}^{\alpha}=\displaystyle{\sum_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}=1}\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha},F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\beta}),

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F_{0}^{A}+e^{B}F_{0}^{B}dv-\bar{n}_{e},

\displaystyle\text{Order ~$O(\varepsilon)$:}\quad\quad\qquad~~~~(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{1}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{1}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\sum_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}=2}\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha},F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\beta}),

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F_{1}^{A}+e^{B}F_{1}^{B}dv,

\displaystyle.\,.\,.

\displaystyle\text{Order ~$O(\varepsilon^{i})$:}\,\quad\quad~~~~~~(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{j}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{j}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle{\mathop{\sum}_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}=i+1\atop i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\geq 0}\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha},F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\beta})-\mathop{\sum}_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}=i\atop i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\geq 1}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i^{\prime}}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{i}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F_{i}^{A}+e^{B}F_{i}^{B}dv,\qquad{i}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+},\quad{i}\geq 2.

Then, the equations governing the remainder term can be expressed as

\left\{\begin{gathered}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{R}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}}(Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{0}^{\alpha},F_{R}^{\beta})+Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{R}^{\alpha},F_{0}^{\beta}))\\
\quad\hskip 36.98866pt=\varepsilon^{k-1}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{R}^{\alpha},F_{R}^{\beta})+\displaystyle{\sum^{2k-1}_{i=1}}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}}\varepsilon^{i-1}(Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i}^{\alpha},F_{R}^{\beta})+Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{R}^{\alpha},F_{i}^{\beta}))\\
\hskip 51.21504pt-\varepsilon^{k}\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\alpha}-\displaystyle{\sum^{2k-1}_{i=1}}\varepsilon^{i}(\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{R}^{\alpha})+\varepsilon^{k-1}E^{\alpha},\\
\Delta\phi_{R}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F_{R}^{A}+e^{B}F_{R}^{B}dv,\end{gathered}\right.

(1.9)

where

\displaystyle E^{\alpha}:=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})F_{2k-1}^{\alpha}+\mathop{\sum}_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}\geq 2k\atop 2k-1\geq i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\geq 1}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}}\varepsilon^{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}-2k}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i^{\prime}}^{\alpha},F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\beta})

(1.10)

\displaystyle-\mathop{\sum}_{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}\geq 2k-1\atop 2k-1\geq i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\geq 0}\varepsilon^{i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}-2k+1}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i^{\prime}}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{\alpha}.

Finally, from the equations
\eqref{ORDERZONG}_{1}
and (
1.5
), we deduce that
\mathbf{F}_{0}
is a local bi-Maxwellian:

\mathbf{F}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}F_{0}^{A}\\
F_{0}^{B}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{n^{A}(m^{A})^{3/2}}{(2\pi\theta)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{m^{A}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{2\theta}}\\
\frac{n^{B}(m^{B})^{3/2}}{(2\pi\theta)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{m^{B}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{2\theta}}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu^{A}\\
\mu^{B}\end{array}\right),

(1.11)

where
n^{\alpha},\mathbf{u},\theta
are the mass, velocity and the temperature,
which will be determined later.

Remark 1.1
.

The reasons for the truncated Hilbert expansion taking the form of (
1.7
) are as follows. Based on the Hilbert expansion

F_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}\varepsilon^{i}F^{\alpha}_{i},

the truncated expansion is assumed to be

F_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\varepsilon^{i}F^{\alpha}_{i}+\varepsilon^{r}F_{R}^{\alpha},\quad n\geq r\geq 1.

(1.12)

It can be seen from Sections 5 and 6 that the validity time of the solution increases as
\frac{r}{n}

decreases, as noted in Remark
4.12
. However, the decrease in
\frac{r}{n}

necessitates the expansion of additional terms, requiring
r\geq 6
, see Remark
4.10
. We consider both the validity time and term expansion requirements. To balance their influences, we choose
r=\frac{n+1}{2}

, which implies
n=2k-1
and
r=k
. This also establishes a balance between the nonlinear term
\sum_{\beta=A,B}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{R}^{\alpha},F_{R}^{\beta})

and (
1.10
), as they share the same coefficient
\varepsilon^{k-1}
. For other coefficients in the remainder expansion, corresponding conclusions can be drawn by following the analysis presented in this paper.

1.4.
Notations

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we use notation
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}
to denote the norm of
L^{p}
space with respect to the variables
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
|\cdot|_{L^{p}_{v}}
and
|\cdot|_{L^{p}_{x}}
are the
L^{p}
norms w.r.t. the variable
v
and
x
respectively. For vector functions
\mathbf{f}(t,x,v)=(f^{A},f^{B})^{T}
, its
L^{p}
norms are denoted by

\|\mathbf{f}\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}=\|f^{A}\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}}+\|f^{B}\|_{L^{p}_{x,v}},\qquad|\mathbf{f}|_{L^{p}_{v}}=|f^{A}|_{L^{p}_{v}}+|f^{B}|_{L^{p}_{v}}.

The notations
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{x,v}
and
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{v}
are the inner product of the function space
L^{2}_{x,v}
and
L^{2}_{v}
respectively. For vector functions
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g}
, their inner product in
L^{2}_{v}
and
L^{2}_{x,v}
are denoted as

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right\rangle_{v}=\left\langle f^{A},g^{A}\right\rangle_{v}+\left\langle f^{B},g^{B}\right\rangle_{v},~~~~\left\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right\rangle_{x,v}=\left\langle f^{A},g^{A}\right\rangle_{x,v}+\left\langle f^{B},g^{B}\right\rangle_{x,v}.

Let
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
be a multi-index. The

s-
th order derivative is
\partial_{x}^{\ell}=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\ell_{2}}\partial_{x_{3}}^{\ell_{3}}
, with
|\ell|=s
.

W_{x}^{s,p}
‌represents the standard Sobolev space with corresponding
norm
\|\cdot\|_{W_{x}^{s,p}}
. Moreover, when
p=2
, we denote

W_{x}^{s,2}
as
H_{x}^{s}
. The letter
C>1
, which may vary from line to line is used to
denote a constant.
J\lesssim K
means
J\leq CK
. If both hold

J\lesssim K
and
K\lesssim J
, we say

J\thicksim K
.
We define the polynomial weight function
\left\langle v\right\rangle=1+|v|
, and the collision frequency function

\upsilon^{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta=A,B}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)\mu^{\beta}(v_{*})d\sigma dv_{*}.

Then for
-3<\gamma\leq 1
, it can be proved that
\upsilon^{\alpha}\thicksim\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}
, so we introduce the following weighted
L^{2}
norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\nu}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f|^{2}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}dxdv,\qquad|f|_{\nu}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f|^{2}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}dv.

Next, we introduce a global bi-Maxwellian

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}_{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu_{M}^{A}\\
\mu_{M}^{B}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\theta_{M})^{3/2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}e^{-\frac{m_{A}|v|^{2}}{2\theta_{M}}}\\
e^{-\frac{m_{B}|v|^{2}}{2\theta_{M}}}\end{array}\right).

From (
2.7
) and (
2.9
), the constant
\bar{\theta}
is defined by

\bar{\theta}=[c_{1}+c_{2}(C_{p})^{\frac{2}{3}}](\bar{n}_{1})^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(1.13)

Without loss of generality, we assume
m^{A}\geq m^{B}
. There exists

\theta_{M}=\frac{2m^{A}}{2m^{A}+m^{B}}\bar{\theta}

such that

\theta_{M}\leq\mathop{\rm min}_{(t,x)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\theta(t,x)\leq\mathop{\rm max}_{(t,x)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\theta(t,x)\leq\frac{m^{A}+m^{B}}{m^{A}}\theta_{M}.

Moreover, based on the bound (
2.8
), there exist constants
C>0
and some

\frac{m^{A}}{m^{A}+m^{B}}<\tilde{q}<1
, such that for each
(t,x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it holds

C^{-1}\mu^{\alpha}_{M}\leq\mu^{\alpha}\leq C(\mu^{\alpha}_{M})^{\tilde{q}}.

Then, we can propose the following weight function:

w_{\gamma}(v)=\begin{cases}&\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}\qquad\,\,\,\gamma=1,\\
&\omega_{\gamma}\,\,\,\quad-1\leq\gamma<1,\\
&\omega_{-1}\quad-3<\gamma<-1.\end{cases}

(1.14)

With fixed
k
from (
1.7
), the weight function satisfies:

\omega_{\gamma}=e^{\tilde{\kappa}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}},\quad\tilde{\kappa}=\kappa_{0}(1+(1+t)^{-\frac{2}{2k-1}}),\quad 0<\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(m^{A},m^{B})\ll 1.

(1.15)

The rationale for constructing the weight function (
1.15
) is detailed in Section 5.

Finally, from the remainder term equations (
1.9
), the
L^{\infty}
weighted functions are given by:

\mathbf{R}_{\gamma}=(R_{\gamma}^{A},R_{\gamma}^{B})^{T}=\begin{cases}&\mathbf{h}=(h^{A},h^{B})^{T}\qquad\,\qquad\,\gamma=1,\\
&\mathbf{S}_{\gamma}=(S^{A}_{\gamma},S^{B}_{\gamma})^{T}\,\quad\quad-1\leq\gamma<1,\\
&\mathbf{S}_{-1}=(S^{A}_{-1},S^{B}_{-1})^{T}\quad-3<\gamma<-1,\end{cases}

(1.16)

here

h^{\alpha}=\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}F_{R}^{\alpha},\quad l\geq 7,\qquad S^{\alpha}_{\gamma}=\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}F_{R}^{\alpha},\quad-1\leq\gamma<1,\quad{\rm for}~~\alpha=A,B.

(1.17)

The validity time is defined by

T_{\gamma}=\begin{cases}&T_{L}\,\,\,\quad-1\leq\gamma\leq 1,\\
&T_{S}\quad-3<\gamma<-1,\end{cases}

(1.18)

where

T_{L}=O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{2(2k-1)}}),\qquad T_{S}=O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{(1-\gamma)(2k-1)}}).

(1.19)

The initial total energy is defined as

\mathcal{E}^{\rm in}=\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\Big(\Big\|\frac{F_{R}^{\rm\alpha,in}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha,in}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+|\nabla_{x}\phi^{\rm in}_{R}|_{{L^{2}_{x}}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2-\gamma}w_{\gamma}F_{R}^{\rm\alpha,in}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha,in}_{M}}}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\varepsilon^{5}\Big\|\nabla_{x,v}(\frac{w_{\gamma}F_{R}^{\rm\alpha,in}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha,in}_{M}}})\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\Big).

1.5.
The main result

Now, we state the main result of this article.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{F}_{0}

be defined in (
1.11
).
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)

is the smooth solution to the hyperbolic system (
2.2
)-(
2.3
) established in Lemma
2.1
. We denote
F_{j}^{\alpha}=f_{j}^{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu}

for
j\geq 1
and
\alpha=A,B
, as obtained in Section 2.2. Suppose that the initial data are given as

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{\varepsilon}^{A}(0,x,v)\\[5.69054pt]
F_{\varepsilon}^{B}(0,x,v)\end{array}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{k}^{A,\rm in}(x,v)\\[5.69054pt]
F_{k}^{B,\rm in}(x,v)\end{array}\right)+\varepsilon^{k}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{R}^{A,\rm in}(x,v)\\[5.69054pt]
F_{R}^{B,\rm in}(x,v)\end{array}\right)\geq 0.

(1.20)

Then, there exists a small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the initial value problem (
1.6
)-(
1.20
) admits a unique solution. The remainder term satisfies:

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{\gamma}}\Big(\Big\|\frac{F_{R}^{\rm\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big\|\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2-\gamma}w_{\gamma}F_{R}^{\rm\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha}_{M}}}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\varepsilon^{5}\big\|\nabla_{x,v}(\frac{w_{\gamma}F_{R}^{\rm\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\rm\alpha}_{M}}})\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\Big)\leq\mathcal{E}^{\rm in}.

(1.21)

Remark 1.2

(
\mathbf{Criteria\,\,for\,\,classification}
)
.

This theorem is divided into three cases: the critical case
(\gamma=1)
, the moderate range
(-1\leq\gamma<1)
, and the strongly singular case
(-3<\gamma<-1)
, with the soft potential analysis requiring the additional constraint of compact initial values.

Remark 1.3

(
\mathbf{Different\,\,weight\,\,functions\,\,for\,\,the\,\,remainder\,\,term}
)
.

‌The rationale for treating the hard sphere model separately stems from the following estimate:

\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\alpha}(t,s)\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{3}(s,X(s),V(s))ds\Big|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon\mathcal{I}_{1})e^{-\frac{\nu_{0}t}{2\varepsilon}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}(e^{\frac{\nu_{0}s}{2\varepsilon}}\|\langle v\rangle^{1-\gamma}\mathbf{h}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}).

Polynomial weights suffice for the
L_{x,v}^{2}
estimation. However, for
-3<\gamma<1
, standard nonlinear estimation techniques fail to handle the linear term
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{3}
(
4.31
) adequately. This necessitates the construction of our new weight function
\omega_{\gamma}
(
1.15
) to transform and control this term.

Remark 1.4

(
\mathbf{Shortening\,\,of\,\,the\,\,validity\,\,time\,\,for\,-3<\gamma<-1}
)
.

Section 5 initially establishes that the weight function

\omega_{\gamma}=e^{\tilde{\kappa}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}}

preserves the time of validity

T_{L}=O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{2(2k-1)}})
. Notably, the specific case

\gamma=-1
yields

e^{\tilde{\kappa}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2}}
. As emphasized in Remark
5.2
, the constraint

\frac{3-\gamma}{2}\leq 2
implies that the solution’s time of validity is necessarily reduced to

T_{S}=O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{(1-\gamma)(2k-1)}})
.

The paper is structured as follows
.
Section 2 derives the macroscopic components
\mathbf{F}_{i}
(
i=0,\dots,2k-1
) evolution equations and establishes uniform a priori estimates.
Section 3 obtains the
L^{2}
-norm estimates of the remainder term through energy method analysis.
Section 4 provides
W_{x,v}^{1,\infty}
estimates for
h^{\alpha}
via Duhamel’s principle, addressing the
\gamma=1
case.
Section 5 extends the analysis to
-1\leq\gamma<1
cases for
S^{\alpha}_{\gamma}
in
W_{x,v}^{1,\infty}
framework.
Section 6 investigates the more singular
-3<\gamma<-1
case, analyzing the shortening of the validity time‌.
Within this
L^{2}
–
W_{x,v}^{1,\infty}
hybrid analytical framework, we ultimately prove Theorem
1.1
, which rigorously establishes the hydrodynamic limit for multi-component VPB systems.

1.6.
Innovations of this article

The innovations and challenges discussed in this paper arise primarily from three aspects: the physical model, the technical aspects, and the conclusions.

\mathbf{1.\,\,The\,\,physical\,\,model.}\,

★

The existing results for Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann (VPB) fluid equations concerning gas mixtures often adopt the normalization assumption where both mass and charge magnitude are set to unity, which inherently carries limitations. In contrast, the physical model considered in this work maintains greater fidelity by preserving the actual ionic masses and charge states. Consequently, our conclusions not only enable accurate modeling of ion collision dynamics in atmospheric thermosphere but also demonstrate broader applicability across various physical and chemical domains.

★

The Euler-Poisson system (
2.2
) derived from the VPB system for gas mixtures (
m^{A}\neq m^{B}
) includes
\rho=m^{A}n^{A}+m^{B}n^{B}
,
\tilde{n}=n^{A}+n^{B}
, and
n_{e}=e^{A}n^{A}+e^{B}n^{B}
. These quantities make it more difficult to prove the existence of solutions for this system.
Furthermore, the equations of the macroscopic part for higher-order systems are linear equations with a coefficient matrix of size
6\times 6
, which is more complex than the equations derived from the VPB system for a single type of gas. It only has a
5\times 5
coefficient matrix.

★

Compared to a single-gas model, gas mixtures with unequal masses exhibit distinct remainder estimates, where the difference in the
L^{2}

estimate is caused by the mathematical structure of the terms
\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\alpha}

and
\Delta\phi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}F^{A}+e^{B}F^{B}\,dv
. While, the structure of the characteristic lines in the
L^{\infty}

estimate is different (if
\frac{e^{A}}{m^{A}}\neq\frac{e^{B}}{m^{B}}

), featuring a combination of two different types of characteristic lines in (
4.35
).

\mathbf{2.\,\,The\,\,technical\,\,aspects.}\,

★

When conducting
L^{\infty}
estimation, we will encounter the following nonlinear term:

\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{3}=-\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}\nabla_{v}(\frac{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}})h^{\alpha},\,\quad|\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{3}|\thicksim\left\langle v\right\rangle\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}.

If
\gamma\neq 1
, the decay provided by the characteristic lines will be insufficient to control its growth in velocity, since

\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\alpha}(t,s)\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}_{3}(s,X(s),V(s))ds\right|\lesssim\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon\mathcal{I}_{1})e^{-\frac{\nu_{o}t}{2\varepsilon}}\displaystyle{\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}}(e^{\frac{\nu_{o}s}{2\varepsilon}}\|\left\langle v\right\rangle^{1-\gamma}\mathbf{h}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}).

To address this, we propose a weight function
\omega_{\gamma}(t,v)
(see (
1.14
) and (
1.15
)) to transform it via the equation:

\begin{split}\hat{\upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}(t,x,v):&=\frac{\upsilon^{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{\omega_{\kappa}(t,v)}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}\nabla_{v}(\frac{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}_{M}}}{\omega_{\kappa}(t,v)})-\frac{\partial_{t}\omega_{\kappa}(t,v)}{\omega_{\kappa}(t,v)}\\
&\geq\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}}{C\varepsilon}-C\left\langle v\right\rangle\|\nabla_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}\kappa_{1}}{C}\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\kappa_{2}}}{(1+t)^{1+\kappa_{1}}}.\end{split}

Young’s inequality is used to transform
\left\langle v\right\rangle
:

\begin{split}\left\langle v\right\rangle&=\Big(\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}\varepsilon^{\iota-1}(1+t)^{\frac{(1+\kappa_{1})(1-\gamma)}{\kappa_{2}-1}}\Big)^{\frac{\kappa_{2}-1}{\kappa_{2}-\gamma}}\Big(\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\kappa_{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{(1-\iota)(\kappa_{2}-1)}{1-\gamma}}(1+t)^{-(1+\kappa_{1})}\Big)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{\kappa_{2}-\gamma}}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}\varepsilon^{\iota}(1+t)^{\frac{(1+\kappa_{1})(1-\gamma)}{\kappa_{2}-1}}+\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\kappa_{2}}\varepsilon^{\frac{(1-\iota)(\kappa_{2}-1)}{1-\gamma}}(1+t)^{-(1+\kappa_{1})}.\end{split}

Through subsequent calculations, with appropriate selection of parameters
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
, and
\iota
(detailed in Scetion 5 (
5.3
)-(
5.6
)), one obtains

\begin{split}\hat{\upsilon_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}&\geq\frac{2\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}}{3C\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{4Ck}\frac{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}}{(1+t)^{\frac{2k+1}{2k-1}}}>\frac{2\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}}{3C\varepsilon}.\end{split}

This effectively helps us overcome the difficulties caused by the electric field effect.‌‌

★

Through a rigorous analysis of the velocity decay properties of the operator
K_{M,w}^{\alpha,c}
(Lemma
4.3
, under the condition
m^{A}\neq m^{B}
), in the local
L_{x,v}^{\infty}
framework, we are able to establish the following estimate:

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|k_{M,\omega_{\gamma}}^{\alpha\beta}(s,X^{\alpha}(s),V^{\alpha}(s),v_{*})k_{M,\omega_{\gamma}}^{\beta\beta^{{}^{\prime}}}(s_{1},X^{\alpha,\beta}_{1}(s_{1}),V^{\alpha,\beta}_{1}(s_{1}),v_{**})|dv_{**}\,dv_{*}\\
&\hskip 179.25244pt\leq\frac{C\left\langle V(s)\right\rangle^{\gamma}}{1+|V(s)|}\times\left\langle V_{1}(s_{1})\right\rangle^{\gamma}.\end{split}

When dealing with soft potentials, we need to ‌squeeze out an
\varepsilon
from the certain nonlinear terms to eliminate‌ the intrinsic

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
, (detailed in Scetion 5 (
5.8
)).

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}_{0}^{\alpha}(t,s)f(t,x,v)ds\right|\lesssim\left|\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}\mathcal{T}_{0}^{\alpha}(t,s)ds\right|\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|\frac{f(s)}{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\lesssim\varepsilon\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|\frac{f(s)}{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{|\gamma|}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}.

\mathbf{3.\,\,The\,\,conclusions.}\,

★

The potential range that we consider is
(-3,1]
, which includes four different cases:
\gamma=1
,
0\leq\gamma<1
,
-1\leq\gamma<0
, and
-3<\gamma<-1
. Moreover, this approach provides a unification of methods for handling different potentials, as previously addressed by others using various techniques. Our findings further quantify the degree of change in polynomial-weighted initial data, which is always
\langle v\rangle^{2-\gamma}

for
\gamma\in\left(-3,1\right]
.

★

We discuss the validity time that depends on the Knudsen number
\varepsilon
. The validity time is
O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{2(2k-1)}})

when
-1\leq\gamma\leq 1
, and it can be
O(\varepsilon^{-\frac{2k-3}{(1-\gamma)(2k-1)}})

when
-3<\gamma<-1
. This conclusion applies to all
k\geq 6
. We note that the validity time is also continuous with respect to the potential
\gamma\in(-3,1]
, see Figure 2.

2.
The determination of
\mathbf{F_{i}}~~(i=0,\cdots,2k-1)
.

2.1.
The equations for the equilibrium
\mathbf{F}_{0}

From (
1.11
), we know
\mathbf{F}_{0}
is a local bi-Maxwellian

\mathbf{F}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}F_{0}^{A}\\
F_{0}^{B}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{n^{A}(m^{A})^{3/2}}{(2\pi\theta)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{m^{A}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{2\theta}}\\
\frac{n^{B}(m^{B})^{3/2}}{(2\pi\theta)^{3/2}}e^{-\frac{m^{B}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{2\theta}}\end{array}\right).

(2.1)

By a direct calculation,
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)
satisfies the following relations:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{0}^{\alpha}dv=n^{\alpha},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}vF_{0}^{\alpha}dv=n^{\alpha}\mathbf{u},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v|^{2}F_{0}^{\alpha}dv=n^{\alpha}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+3n^{\alpha}\theta,

and we denote

\rho=m^{A}n^{A}+m^{B}n^{B},\quad\tilde{n}=n^{A}+n^{B},\quad n_{e}=e^{A}n^{A}+e^{B}n^{B}.

The first line of the equation
\eqref{ORDERZONG}_{1}
can be written in a vector form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{0}^{A}\\
(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{0}^{B}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{i+j=1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}Q^{AA}(F_{i}^{A},F_{j}^{A})+Q^{AB}(F_{i}^{A},F_{j}^{B})\\
Q^{BA}(F_{i}^{B},F_{j}^{A})+Q^{BB}(F_{i}^{B},F_{j}^{B})\end{array}\right).

Moreover, taking inner product with six collision invariants
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2},v_{1}\mathbf{m},v_{2}\mathbf{m},v_{3}\mathbf{m},|v|^{2}\mathbf{m}\}
(see (
2.16
)), one can
obtain the system of
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}n^{A}+{\rm div}(n^{B}\mathbf{u})=0,\\
\partial_{t}n^{B}+{\rm div}(n^{B}\mathbf{u})=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{u})+{\rm div}(\rho\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{u})+\nabla(\tilde{n}\theta)-n_{e}\nabla\phi_{0}=0,\\
\partial_{t}[\frac{\rho}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\tilde{n}\theta]+{\rm div}\left[(\frac{\rho}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\tilde{n}\theta)\mathbf{u}+\tilde{n}\theta\mathbf{u}\right]-n_{e}\nabla\phi_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}=0,\\
\Delta\phi_{0}=n_{e}-\bar{n}_{e},\end{array}\right.

(2.2)

with initial data

[n^{A}(x),n^{B}(x),\mathbf{u}(x),\theta(x)]|_{t=0}=[n^{A,in}(x),n^{B,in}(x),\mathbf{u}^{in}(x),\theta^{in}(x)].

(2.3)

Further, by replacing
\eqref{EQF0EPSION}_{4}
with

(\eqref{EQF0EPSION}_{4}-\eqref{EQF0EPSION}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{u})
, and
utilizing
\eqref{EQF0EPSION}_{1}
and
\eqref{EQF0EPSION}_{2}
, the
system
(
2.2
)
can be written as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}n^{A}+{\rm div}(n^{A}\mathbf{u})=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}n^{B}+{\rm div}(n^{B}\mathbf{u})=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\rho\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+\rho(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}+\nabla(\tilde{n}\theta)-n_{e}\nabla\phi_{0}=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}\theta+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\theta+\frac{2}{3}\theta{\rm div}\mathbf{u}=0,\\
\Delta\phi_{0}=n_{e}-\bar{n}_{e}.\end{array}\right.

(2.4)

Denote
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)^{T}
, and
\mathbf{V}_{0}=(0,0,\nabla\phi_{0},0)^{T}
. The system
(
2.4
) could be changed into the following form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}_{0}-\frac{n_{e}}{\rho}\mathbf{V}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathbf{A}_{j}(\mathbf{U}_{0})\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbf{U}_{0}=0,

(2.5)

where

\mathbf{A}_{j}(\mathbf{U}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}u^{j}&0&n^{A}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&0\\
0&u^{j}&n^{B}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&0\\
\frac{\theta}{\rho}\mathbf{e}_{j}&\frac{\theta}{\rho}\mathbf{e}_{j}&u^{j}\mathbf{I}_{3}&\frac{\tilde{n}}{\rho}\mathbf{e}_{j}\\
0&0&\frac{2}{3}\theta\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&u^{j}\end{pmatrix},

with the standard vectors
e_{1}=(1,0,0)^{T},~e_{2}=(0,1,0)^{T},~e_{3}=(0,0,1)^{T}
and
\mathbf{I}_{3}={\rm diag}(1\,1\,1)
. As stated in
[
31
]
, the system (
2.5
) is well-posedness in local time if it is a symmetrizable hyperbolic system. We can find a positive defined symmetric matrix:

\mathbf{A}_{0}(\mathbf{U}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\theta}{n^{A}}&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{\theta}{n^{B}}&0&0\\
0&0&\rho\mathbf{I}_{3}&0\\
0&0&0&\frac{3}{2}\frac{\tilde{n}}{\theta}\end{pmatrix},

and multiply (
2.5
) by
\mathbf{A}_{0}(\mathbf{U}_{0})
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{0}(\mathbf{U}_{0})(\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}_{0}-\frac{n_{e}}{\rho}\mathbf{V}_{0})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathbf{A}_{0}(\mathbf{U}_{0})\mathbf{A}_{j}(\mathbf{U}_{0})\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbf{U}_{0}=0.

(2.6)

It can be verified that

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{A}_{0}(\mathbf{U}_{0})\mathbf{A}_{j}(\mathbf{U}_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{u^{j}\theta}{n^{A}}&0&\theta\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&0\\
0&\frac{u^{j}\theta}{n^{B}}&\theta\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&0\\
\theta\mathbf{e}_{j}&\theta\mathbf{e}_{j}&\rho u^{j}\mathbf{I}_{3}&\tilde{n}\mathbf{e}_{j}\\
0&0&\tilde{n}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}&\frac{3}{2}\frac{\tilde{n}}{\theta}u^{j}\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{gathered}\quad j=1,2,3.

Since the matrices in (
2.6
) are symmetric,
according to the classical mathematical theory of hyperbolic systems
(see
[
31
]
), there exists a local in-time solution in
the corresponding Sobolev space. However, finding a global solution
to the Euler-Poisson system is a very difficult task. Even for the
fluid system derived from the VPB equations of a single gas particle
remains a challenging issue. In
[
29
]
, T. Makino worked
out a local solution with compact support for the initial data.
However, what we need is a global-in-time solution. The equilibrium
cannot be vacuum. The existing conclusions regarding global-in-time
solutions without compact support of initial data, including the
case of
p=\rho^{\frac{5}{3}}
, always impose additional
structural conditions, such as the irrotational condition in

[
13
]
and the spherical symmetry condition in

[
6
]
. Unfortunately, due to the quality and arbitrary
selection of the charge
e^{\alpha}
, the non-homogeneous system of
equations cannot maintain these additional structural conditions
like a single equation. Thus, the existing relevant results cannot
be utilized. Currently, the only feasible method is to consider the
initial data for
n^{A}
and
n^{B}
in a certain proportion. This
allows us to transform the full Euler-Poisson system into the
isentropic Euler-Poisson system, thereby utilizing the results in

[
13
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let initial data
n^{\rm B,in}=C_{p}n^{\rm A,in}
and
n^{\rm A,in}-\bar{n}_{1},n^{\rm B,in}-\bar{n}_{2},\mathbf{u}^{in}
be sufficiently small, where
\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2}=C_{p}\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{e}=e^{A}\bar{n}_{1}+e^{B}\bar{n}_{2},
are constants. They satisfy following conditions:

\nabla\times\mathbf{u}^{in}=0,\qquad\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(n_{e}^{in}-\bar{n}_{e})dx=0.

(2.7)

Then, there exist a unique global solution

(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)
, where
\frac{1}{C}<\theta<C
, and
n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\phi_{0}
satisfy the following pointwise decay

\|n^{A}-\bar{n}_{1}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}+\|n^{B}-\bar{n}_{2}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}+\|\mathbf{u}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}+\|\nabla\phi_{0}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}\leq\frac{C}{(1+t)^{q}},

(2.8)

for each
s\geq 0
and
1<q<\frac{1}{3}
.

Proof.

Let
n^{B}(t,x)=P(t,x)n^{A}(t,x)
. From
\eqref{REWEUPSIO}_{2}
, one can get

\partial_{t}P+u\cdot\nabla_{x}P=0,\\

with initial data
P(0,x)=C_{p}
. It is easy to solve
P(t,x)\equiv C_{p}
.
We note that
the continuity equation for
n^{A},n^{B}
and the temperature equation for
\theta
are equivalent when

\theta=c_{1}(n^{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}+c_{2}(n^{B})^{\frac{2}{3}}=[c_{1}+c_{2}(C_{p})^{\frac{2}{3}}](n^{A})^{\frac{2}{3}},

(2.9)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are two constants. We plunge (
2.9
) into (
2.4
),
then they satisfy the following isentropic Euler-Poisson system:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}n^{A}+{\rm div}(n^{A}\mathbf{u})=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}+(\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}+\frac{\nabla[(1+C_{p})(c_{1}+c_{2}C_{p}^{\frac{2}{3}})(n^{A})^{\frac{5}{3}}]}{(m^{A}+C_{p}m^{B})n^{A}}-\frac{e^{A}+e^{B}C_{p}}{m^{A}+C_{p}m^{B}}\nabla\phi_{0}=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\Delta\phi_{0}=(e^{A}+e^{B}C_{p})n^{A}-(e^{A}+e^{B}C_{p})\bar{n}_{1}.\end{array}\right.

Since
c_{1},c_{2}
and
C_{p}
are constants, it holds that

\nabla\times\Big\{\frac{\nabla[(1+C_{p})(c_{1}+c_{2}C_{p}^{\frac{2}{3}})(n^{A})^{\frac{5}{3}}]}{(m^{A}+C_{p}m^{B})n^{A}}-\frac{e^{A}+e^{B}C_{p}}{m^{A}+C_{p}m^{B}}\nabla\phi_{0}\Big\}\equiv 0.

Therefore, we can use the proof in
[
13
]
. ∎

Remark 2.2
.

There are also some results concerning the Euler-Poisson equations for mixed fluids, such as
[
16
]
. However, these fluid equations are not directly derived from Vlasov–Poisson-Boltzmann equations. The fundamental reason lies in the fact that the equilibrium state of gas mixture derived from momentum conservation and energy conservation, which determines special forms of collision invariants. So it is hard to ‌decouple‌ the momentum equations and energy equations for the two different fluids.

To accommodate additional symbols in the following section, we temporarily repurpose the letter
l
, distinct from its usage in (
1.17
).

2.2.
The construction of
F^{\alpha}_{l}
(
\alpha\in\{A,B\}
,
l=1,2,\cdots,2k-1
)

First, we introduce the linearized collision operator and discuss some of its necessary properties.

Let
\mathbf{F}_{l}=(F^{A}_{l},F^{B}_{l})^{T}=(\sqrt{\mu^{A}}f^{A}_{l},\sqrt{\mu^{B}}f^{B}_{l})^{T}
,
\mathbf{f}_{l}=(f^{A}_{l},f^{B}_{l})^{T}
, and we can define the linearized bi-collision operator
\mathbf{L}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}\mathbf{f}_{l}=\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbf{L}^{A}\mathbf{f}_{l}\\
\mathbf{L}^{B}\mathbf{f}_{l}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{A}}}\Big(Q^{AA}(\mu^{A},\sqrt{\mu^{A}}f_{l}^{A})+Q^{AA}(\sqrt{\mu^{A}}f_{l}^{A},\mu^{A})\\
\hskip 28.45274pt+Q^{AB}(\sqrt{\mu^{A}}f_{l}^{A},\mu^{B})+Q^{AB}(\mu^{A},\sqrt{\mu^{B}}f_{l}^{B})\Big)\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{B}}}\Big(Q^{BA}(\mu^{B},\sqrt{\mu^{A}}f_{l}^{A})+Q^{BA}(\sqrt{\mu^{B}}f_{l}^{B},\mu^{A})\\
\hskip 28.45274pt+Q^{BB}(\mu^{B},\sqrt{\mu^{B}}f_{l}^{B})+Q^{BB}(\sqrt{\mu^{B}}f_{l}^{B},\mu^{B})\Big)\end{array}\right).

Here
\mathbf{L}
is a self-adjoint operator
[
8
]
in
(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R})^{3})^{2}
. It could be decomposed as
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{\nu}+K
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{\nu}\mathbf{f}_{l}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\nu^{A}(v)f_{l}^{A}\\
\nu^{B}(v)f_{l}^{B}\end{array}\right),\quad K\mathbf{f}_{l}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}K^{A}\mathbf{f}_{l}\\
K^{B}\mathbf{f}_{l}\end{array}\right),

where the collision frequency
\nu
and the
K
operator are defined by

\begin{split}&\nu^{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta=A,B}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)\mu^{\beta}_{*}d\sigma dv_{*},\\
&\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}_{l}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}\sum_{\beta=A,B}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}B^{\alpha\beta}(|v-v_{*}|,\cos\theta)\Big[\mu^{\alpha}(v^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu^{\beta}(v_{*}^{\prime})}f_{l}^{\beta}(v_{*}^{\prime})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\mu^{\beta}(v_{*}^{\prime})\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}(v^{\prime})}\,f_{l}^{\alpha}(v^{\prime})-\mu^{\alpha}(v)\sqrt{\mu^{\beta}(v_{*})}f_{l}^{\beta}(v_{*})\Big]d\sigma dv_{*}.\\
\end{split}

(The kernel of
\mathbf{L}

[
8
]
).
\langle\mathbf{f}_{l},\mathbf{L}\mathbf{f}_{l}\rangle_{(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R})^{3})^{2}}=0
if and only if
\mathbf{f}_{l}
belongs to the kernel of
\mathbf{L}
, which is denoted as
{\rm Ker}(\mathbf{L})
. It is spanned by the following six vector functions

{\rm Ker}(\mathbf{L})={\rm span}\{X_{0},X_{1},\cdots,X_{5}\},

with

\begin{split}&\mathbf{X}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu^{A}}{n^{A}}}\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{X}_{1}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu^{B}}{n^{B}}}\mathbf{e}_{2},\,\mathbf{X}_{j}=\frac{v_{j-1}-u^{j-1}}{\sqrt{\theta n}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}m^{A}\sqrt{\mu^{A}}\\[5.69054pt]
m^{B}\sqrt{\mu^{B}}\end{array}\right)~(j=2,3,4),\\
&\mathbf{X}_{5}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6n}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\frac{m^{A}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{\theta}-3)\sqrt{\mu^{A}}\\[5.69054pt]
(\frac{m^{B}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{\theta}-3)\sqrt{\mu^{B}}\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(2.16)

One can check that

\langle\mathbf{X}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},\,\,0\leq i,j\leq 5
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol.
The space can be decomposed by
(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}={\rm Ker}\mathbf{L}\oplus\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L})^{\bot}
, where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L})^{\bot}
is the orthogonal set of
{\rm Ker}\mathbf{L}
.
For any
\mathbf{g}=(g^{A},g^{B})^{T}\in(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}
, it could be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}=\mathcal{P}\mathbf{g}+(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{g},\quad{\rm with}\quad\mathcal{P}\mathbf{g}=\sum_{j=0}^{5}\langle\mathbf{g},\mathbf{X}_{j}\rangle\mathbf{X}_{j},

(2.17)

where
\mathcal{P}\mathbf{g}
and
(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{P}\mathbf{g}
are the macro-micro parts of
\mathbf{g}
respectively.

(The solvability of
\mathbf{L}
). For any
{\mathbf{\bar{R}}}=(\bar{R}^{A},\bar{R}^{B})^{T}\in(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{2})^{2}
, there is a solution of

\mathbf{L}\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{\bar{R}}

if and only if
\mathbf{\bar{R}}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L})^{\bot}
, that is,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{\bar{R}},\mathbf{X}_{j}\rangle_{(L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R})^{3})^{2}}=0,\quad{\rm for~all}\quad j=0,\cdots,5.

(2.18)

The solution
\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L})^{\bot}
is formulated as

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{\bar{R}}.

Now, we can determine the terms
\mathbf{F}_{l}(l=1,2,\cdots,2k-1)
step by step. By macro-micro decomposition in (
2.17
), we write
\mathbf{f}_{l}=(f^{A}_{l},f^{B}_{l})^{T}
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{l}=\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{l}+(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{l}.

The macroscopic part of
\mathbf{f}_{l}
is expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{l}=&\frac{n^{A}_{l}}{\sqrt{n^{A}}}\mathbf{X}_{0}+\frac{n^{B}_{l}}{\sqrt{n^{B}}}\mathbf{X}_{1}+\sum_{j=2,3,4}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\theta}}u_{l}^{j-1}\mathbf{X}_{j}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{6}}\frac{\theta_{l}}{\theta}\mathbf{X}_{5}\\
=&\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\Big(\frac{n^{A}_{l}}{n^{A}}+\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\frac{m^{A}(v-\mathbf{u})}{\theta}+\frac{\theta_{l}}{6\theta}(\frac{m^{A}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{\theta}-3)\Big)\sqrt{\mu^{A}}\\
\Big(\frac{n^{B}_{l}}{n^{B}}+\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\frac{m^{B}(v-\mathbf{u})}{\theta}+\frac{\theta_{l}}{6\theta}(\frac{m^{B}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{\theta}-3)\Big)\sqrt{\mu^{B}}\end{array}\right),\end{split}

where
(n_{l}^{A},n_{l}^{B},\mathbf{u}_{l},\theta_{l})
are density of the mass, velocity and temperature, which satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&n_{l}=n_{l}^{A}+n_{l}^{B},\,n_{l}^{\alpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{l}^{\alpha}dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{l}^{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}dv,\quad l\geq 1,\\
&\rho_{l}=m^{A}n_{l}^{A}+m^{B}n_{l}^{B},\,\,n_{l}^{\alpha}u^{j}+n^{\alpha}u^{j}_{l}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{j}F_{l}^{\alpha}dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{j}f_{l}^{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}dv,\\
&n^{\alpha}\theta_{l}+3\theta n^{\alpha}_{l}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}m^{\alpha}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}F_{l}^{\alpha}dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}m^{\alpha}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}f_{l}^{\alpha}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}dv.\end{split}

Moreover, applying (
2.2
) to
\eqref{ORDERZONG}_{3}
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{L}^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}_{l+1}=&-(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})F_{l}^{\alpha}-\nabla_{x}\phi_{l}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{0}^{\alpha}-\mathop{\sum}_{i+j=l\atop i,j\geq 1}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j}^{\alpha}\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\displaystyle{\sum_{i+j=l+1\atop i,j\geq 1}\sum_{\beta=A,B}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(F_{i}^{\alpha},F_{j}^{\beta}):=\mathbf{\bar{R}}^{\alpha}_{l},\end{aligned}

where
\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}=(\mathbf{\bar{R}}^{A}_{l},\mathbf{\bar{R}}^{B}_{l})^{T}
, the solvability condition (
2.18
) means that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l},\mathbf{X}_{j}\right\rangle_{v}=0,\quad{\rm for~all}\quad j=0,\cdots,5.

(2.19)

Then, these six equations in (
2.19
) lead to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllll}\partial_{t}n^{A}_{{l}}+{\rm div}_{x}(n^{A}_{l}\mathbf{u}+n^{A}\mathbf{u}_{l})=0,\\
\partial_{t}n^{B}_{l}+{\rm div}_{x}(n^{B}_{l}\mathbf{u}+n^{B}\mathbf{u}_{l})=0,\\
\rho\left(\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{l}+\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}_{l}\right)-\Big[\frac{n^{A}_{l}}{n^{A}}\nabla_{x}(n^{A}\theta)+\frac{n^{B}_{l}}{n^{B}}\nabla_{x}(n^{B}\theta)\Big]\\
\hskip 42.67912pt-n_{e}\nabla_{x}\phi_{l}+\nabla\Big(\frac{n^{A}\theta_{l}+3\theta n_{l}^{A}}{3}\Big)+\nabla\Big(\frac{n^{B}\theta_{l}+3\theta n_{l}^{B}}{3}\Big)=\mathbf{H}_{l-1},\\
n\Big(\partial_{t}\theta_{l}+\frac{2}{3}(\theta_{l}{\rm div}\mathbf{u}+3\theta{\rm div}\mathbf{u}_{l})+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta_{l}+3\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta\Big)={\rm g}_{l-1},\end{array}\right.

(2.20)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{H}_{l-1})_{i}=&-\sum_{\alpha=A,B}m^{\alpha}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{x_{j}}\Big(\frac{\theta}{m^{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{i,j}f_{l}^{\alpha}dv\Big)+\mathop{\sum}_{i+j=l\atop i,j\geq 1}\rho_{j}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i},\\
{\rm g}_{l-1}=&-2\sum_{\alpha=A,B}m^{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{x_{i}}\Big((\frac{\theta}{m^{\alpha}})^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{B}^{\alpha}_{i}f_{k}^{\alpha}dv+\sum_{j=1}^{3}u_{j}\frac{\theta}{m^{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{i,j}f_{l}^{\alpha}dv\Big)\\
&-2\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{H}_{l-1}+\mathop{\sum}_{i+j=l\atop i,j\geq 1}(\rho_{j}\mathbf{u}+\rho\mathbf{u}_{j})\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}.\end{aligned}

(2.21)

Here the the Burnett functions
\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{i,j},\mathbf{B}^{\alpha}_{i}(\alpha=A,B)
are defined as follows

\begin{split}\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{i,j}:=&\Big(\frac{m^{\alpha}(v_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{i})(v_{j}-\mathbf{u}_{j})}{\theta}-\delta_{ij}\frac{m^{\alpha}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{3\theta}\Big)\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}},\\
\mathbf{B}^{\alpha}_{i}:=&\frac{v_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{i}}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m^{\alpha}}{\theta}}(\frac{m^{\alpha}|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}}{\theta}-5)\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}.\end{split}

It should be pointed out that
\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}_{i,j}\in\mathcal{N}^{\bot}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}^{\bot}
, so the source term
\mathbf{H}_{l-1}
and
\rm{g}_{l-1}
in (
2.21
) depend only on the microscopic part of
(f_{l}^{A},f_{l}^{B})^{T}
.
Finally, the micro-part of
\mathbf{f}_{l}
could be expressed as

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{l+1}=\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
27
]
,
[
39
]
)
.

Let
-3<\gamma\leq 1
and
\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{L})^{\perp}
.

(I)
\mathbf{The\,\,cases}

-\frac{3}{2}<\gamma\leq 1:

Let
0<q<1
be arbitrarily fixed. Define the positive number
k_{\gamma}
by

k_{\gamma}=\left\{\begin{split}&\frac{3-\gamma}{2},\quad if\quad 0\leq\gamma\leq 1,\\
&\frac{3-2\gamma}{2},\quad if\quad-\frac{3}{2}\leq\gamma\leq 0.\end{split}\right.

Assume that
(1+|v|)^{k_{\gamma}}\mu^{-\frac{q}{2}}\in L^{\infty}
. Then the following statements hold:

(i)   For the hard potential cases
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{g}(v)|\leq C\|(1+|v|)^{k_{\gamma}}\mu^{-\frac{q}{2}}\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathbf{L}_{v}^{\infty}}\mu^{\frac{q}{2}}(v),\quad v\in\mathbb{{R}}^{3}.

(2.22)

(ii)   For the part of soft potential cases
-\frac{3}{2}<\gamma<0
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{g}(v)|\leq C\|(1+|v|)^{k_{\gamma}}\mu^{-\frac{q}{2}}\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathbf{L}_{v}^{\infty}}\nu^{-1}(v)\mu^{\frac{q}{2}}(v),\quad v\in\mathbb{{R}}^{3}.

(2.23)

(II)
\mathbf{The\,\,cases}

-3<\gamma\leq-\frac{3}{2}:

Assume that

\displaystyle\sum_{|\beta|\leq 2}\|(1+|v|)^{\gamma(|\beta|-3)}\mu^{-q_{0}}\partial^{\beta}_{v}\mathbf{g}\|_{L_{v}^{2}}<\infty,

for a sufficiently small
0<q_{0}<1
. Then

\displaystyle|\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{g}(v)|\leq C\sum_{|\beta|\leq 2}\|(1+|v|)^{\gamma(|\beta|-3)}\mu^{-q_{0}}\partial^{\beta}_{v}\mathbf{g}\|_{L_{v}^{2}}\mu^{q_{0}}(v),\quad v\in\mathbb{{R}}^{3}.

(2.24)

The positive constant
C>0
above depends on the solution of the compressible Euler systems.

Remark 2.4
.

If we take
\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}
in (
2.2
) to replace
\mathbf{g}
in (
2.24
), then there exists a series of number
10<h_{1}<h_{2}<\cdots<h_{2k-1}
, such that for
|\beta|<2
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{\beta}_{v}\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}|\leq C\{1+|v|\}^{h_{l}}|\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}|\qquad for\quad v\in\mathbb{{R}}^{3}.

Since
\max\{\gamma(|\beta|-3),k_{\gamma}\}\leq 10
, and
\nu^{-1}\leq C\{1+|v|\}^{3}
from (
2.22
),(
2.23
) and (
2.24
), there always holds for all the case
-3<\gamma\leq 1

\displaystyle|\mathbf{L}^{-1}\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}(v)|\leq C\|(1+|v|)^{h_{l}}\mu^{-q_{l}}\mathbf{\bar{R}}_{l}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\{1+|v|\}^{3}\mu^{q_{l}}(v)\quad v\in\mathbb{{R}}^{3},

(2.25)

where
0<q_{0}<1
is small enough and
0<q_{2k-1}<q_{4}<...<q_{1}<q_{0}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta,\nabla\phi_{0})
be the smooth solution of established in Lemma
2.1
.
(\rho^{in}_{l},\mathbf{u}^{in}_{l},\theta^{in}_{l})
is the initial data of the linear system (
2.20
), then it has a unique solution such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\mathbf{f}_{l}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1}(1+|v|)^{h_{l}}\sqrt{\mu},\quad|\nabla_{x}\phi_{1}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1},\qquad\qquad\\
|\nabla_{v}\mathbf{f}_{l}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1}(1+|v|)^{h_{l}+1}\sqrt{\mu},\quad|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{f}_{l}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1}(1+|v|)^{h_{l}+2}\sqrt{\mu},\\
|\nabla^{2}_{v}\mathbf{f}_{l}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1}(1+|v|)^{h_{l}+2}\sqrt{\mu},\quad|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}\mathbf{f}_{l}|\leq C(1+t)^{l-1}(1+|v|)^{h_{l}+3}\sqrt{\mu}.\end{split}

(2.26)

Proof. The proof of the decay estimate could be divided into two parts: the estimate of the macro part
\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{l}
and the estimate of micro part
(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{l}
. By Lemma
2.3
, if (
2.26
) holds for the case
l-1
, one gets

|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{l+1}|\leq C(1+t)^{l}(1+|v|)^{h_{l+1}}\sqrt{\mu}.

To estimate
\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{l}
, noting that its coefficients
(n_{l}^{A},n_{l}^{B},\mathbf{u}_{l},\theta_{l},\nabla\phi_{l})
are solutions of system (
2.20
), and recalling the notations:

\displaystyle\rho=m^{A}n^{A}+m^{B}n^{B},\quad\tilde{n}=n^{A}+n^{B},\quad n_{e}=e^{A}n^{A}+e^{B}n^{B},

we can define

\displaystyle p^{\alpha}_{k}=\frac{n^{\alpha}\theta_{k}+3\theta n^{\alpha}_{k}}{3},\quad p^{\alpha}=n^{\alpha}\theta.

Then the system (
2.20
) is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{split}&\partial_{t}p^{A}_{l}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}p^{A}_{l}+\frac{5}{3}\Big(p^{A}{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}_{l}+p^{A}_{l}{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}\Big)+\nabla_{x}p^{A}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{l}=\frac{n^{A}}{3\tilde{n}}g_{l-1},\\
&\partial_{t}p^{B}_{l}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}p^{B}_{l}+\frac{5}{3}\Big(p^{B}{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}_{l}+p^{B}_{k}{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}\Big)+\nabla_{x}p^{B}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{k}=\frac{n^{B}}{3\tilde{n}}g_{l-1},\\
&\partial_{t}\mathbf{u}_{l}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}_{l}+\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}-\frac{\nabla_{x}p^{A}}{\rho p^{A}}p^{A}_{l}-\frac{\nabla_{x}p^{B}}{\rho p^{B}}p^{B}_{l}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla_{x}(p^{A}_{l}+p^{B}_{l})-\frac{n_{e}}{\rho}\nabla_{x}\phi_{l}-\frac{\nabla_{x}\tilde{p}}{3\rho\theta}\theta_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{H}_{l-1}}{\rho},\\
&\partial_{t}\theta_{l}+\frac{2}{3}(\theta_{l}{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}+3\theta{\rm div}_{x}\mathbf{u}_{l})+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta_{k}+3\mathbf{u}_{l}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta=\frac{g_{l-1}}{\tilde{n}}.\end{split}\right.

(2.27)

Further, setting the two vectors
\mathbf{U}_{l}=(p_{l}^{A},p_{l}^{B},\mathbf{u}_{l},\theta_{l})^{T}
,
\mathbf{V}_{l}=(0,0,\nabla_{x}\phi_{l},0)^{T}
, we symmetrize the linear system (
2.27
) with the matrix
B_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{0}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}_{l}-\frac{n_{e}}{\rho}\mathbf{V}_{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\mathbf{B}_{i}\partial_{i}\mathbf{U}_{l}+\mathbf{F}_{l-1}\mathbf{U}_{l}=\mathbf{G}_{l-1},

(2.28)

where these matrixes are

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{B}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{9}{5n^{A}}&0&0&0&0&-1\\
0&\frac{9}{5n^{B}}&0&0&0&-1\\
0&0&p&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&p&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&p&0\\
-1&-1&0&0&0&\frac{5}{6}\tilde{n}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{9u^{1}}{5n^{A}}&0&\theta&0&0&-u^{1}\\
0&\frac{9u^{1}}{5n^{B}}&\theta&0&0&-u^{1}\\
\theta&\theta&pu^{1}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&pu^{1}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&pu^{1}&0\\
-u^{1}&-u^{1}&0&0&0&\frac{5}{6}\tilde{n}u^{1}\end{pmatrix},\end{gathered}

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{B}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{9u^{2}}{5n^{A}}&0&0&\theta&0&-u^{2}\\
0&\frac{9u^{2}}{5n^{B}}&0&\theta&0&-u^{2}\\
0&0&pu^{2}&0&0&0\\
\theta&\theta&0&pu^{2}&0&0\\
0&0&0&0&pu^{2}&0\\
-u^{2}&-u^{2}&0&0&0&\frac{5}{6}\tilde{n}u^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{9u^{3}}{5n^{A}}&0&0&0&\theta&-u^{3}\\
0&\frac{9u^{3}}{5n^{B}}&0&0&\theta&-u^{3}\\
0&0&pu^{3}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&pu^{3}&0&0\\
\theta&\theta&0&0&pu^{3}&0\\
-u^{3}&-u^{3}&0&0&0&\frac{5}{6}\tilde{n}u^{3}\end{pmatrix}.\end{gathered}

Since
B_{0},B_{1},B_{2}
and
B_{3}

are symmetrized matrices, and
B_{0}

is positive definite, its well-posedness follows from the linear theory in
[
31
]
. First, we consider the case
l=1
. The matrix
\mathbf{F}_{0}

and the source term
\mathbf{G}_{0}

consist of
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)

and their first derivatives. In particular, for any
1<q<\frac{3}{2}

and
s>0
, there exist constants
C_{s}

such that:

\|\mathbf{F}_{0}\|_{W^{s_{0},\infty}}+\|\mathbf{G}_{0}\|_{W^{s_{0},\infty}}\leq\frac{C_{s_{0}}}{(1+t)^{q}}.

Due to
\Delta\phi_{1}=e^{A}n_{1}^{A}+e^{B}n_{1}^{B}
,
we can apply the standard energy method of the linear symmetric hyperbolic system to system (
2.28
). Thus, the following energy inequality yields:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(\|\mathbf{U}_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{1}}})\leq\frac{C_{s_{1}}}{(1+t)^{q}}(\|\mathbf{U}_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{1}}}+\|\mathbf{U}_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1}}}),

(2.29)

where we have used the fact that
\frac{1}{C}<\theta<C
to deduce
\|\mathbf{U}_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{1}\|_{H^{s_{1}}}\leq C
. Therefore, the inequality (
2.26
) has been proved for
l=1
. Next, we suppose that (
2.26
) holds for
1\leq l\leq n
, and consider the case
l=n+1
. By Lemma
2.3
and the induction hypothesis, the microscopic part of
\mathbf{f}_{n+1}
is bounded by

|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{n+1}|\leq C(1+t)^{n}(1+|v|)^{h_{n+1}}\sqrt{\mu}.

While for the macroscopic part
\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{n+1}
, we
need to obtain
p_{n+1}^{A},p_{n+1}^{B},\mathbf{u}_{n+1}
and

\theta_{n+1}
in equation (
2.27
). The structure of the
left-hand side of equation (
2.27
) is same as that of

p_{1}^{A},p_{1}^{B},\mathbf{u}_{1},\theta_{1}
, except for the extra
term caused by
\mathop{\sum}_{i+j=l\atop i,j\geq 1}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j}^{\alpha}
. This term is part of the source term
consisting of
\mathbf{f}_{n+1}
for
1\leq l\leq n
. By the
induction hypothesis, one obtains the following energy inequality
for
\mathbf{U}_{n+1}
and
\mathbf{V}_{n+1}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}(\|\mathbf{U}_{n+1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{n+1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{n+1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{n+1}}})\leq&\frac{C_{s_{n+1}}}{(1+t)^{q}}(\|\mathbf{U}_{n+1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{n+1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{n+1}\|^{2}_{H^{s_{n+1}}})\\
&+C_{s_{n+1}}(1+t)^{n-1}(\|\mathbf{U}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s_{n+1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s_{n+1}}}).\end{split}

By the Gronwall inequality, one gets

\|\mathbf{U}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s_{n+1}}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{n+1}\|_{H^{s_{n+1}}}\leq C_{s_{n+1}}(1+t)^{n}.

Therefore, the case for
l=n+1
has been proved.

3.
The
L^{2}
estimate of the remainder term

In this section, we derive
L^{2}
-energy estimates for the remainder term in both hard and soft potential cases. Before presenting the proof, the following two lemmas are necessary.

Lemma 3.1

(The coercivity of
\mathbf{L}
)
.

For the linearized bi-collision operator
\mathbf{L}
defined in (
2.2
),
there exist
c_{0}>0
, such that

\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\left\langle\mathbf{L}^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}_{i},f^{\alpha}_{i}\right\rangle_{v}\geq c_{0}|(1+|v|)^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{i}|_{\nu}^{2}.

(3.1)

Proof.

‌The proof of this theorem needs to be divided into two cases: hard potential and soft potential. The proof for the hard potential case is in Paper
[
8
]
, while the proof for the soft potential case can be found in my latest Paper
[
38
]
.
∎

The (non-symmetric) bilinear form
\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}

is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(g_{1},g_{2})\triangleq&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}Q^{\alpha\beta}(\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}g_{1},\sqrt{\mu^{\beta}}g_{2})\\
=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|v-v_{*}|^{\gamma}b^{\alpha\beta}(\theta)\\
&\times(\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}(v^{\prime})\mu^{\beta}(v_{*}^{\prime})}g_{1}(v^{\prime})g_{2}(v_{*}^{\prime})-\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}(v)\mu^{\beta}(v_{*})}g_{1}(v)g_{2}(v_{*}))d\sigma dv_{*}.\end{split}

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
-3<\gamma\leq 1
. For the weight function
w_{\gamma}(v)
defined in (
1.14
), there exists a constant
C
such that the operator
\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}

can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\left\langle\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(g_{1},g_{2}),g_{3}\right\rangle_{x,v}\right|\leq&C\|wg_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\,\|g_{3-i}\|_{\nu}\,\|g_{3}\|_{\nu},\quad{\rm for}~~i=1,2.\end{split}

(3.2)

Moreover, there exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\left\langle\Gamma^{\beta\alpha}(g_{1},g_{2}),g_{3}\right\rangle_{x,v}\right|\leq&C(\|wg_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\,\|g_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\|g_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\,\|wg_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}})\,\|g_{3}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}.\end{split}

Proof.

Since for any
l\geq 7
, and
-1\leq\gamma<1
, there exists some constant
C
such that
C\omega_{\gamma}>\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}
, the proof is same as Lemma 6.1 in
[
39
]
, and can be omitted.
∎

From (
1.9
),
\mathbf{f}_{R}=(f_{R}^{A},f_{R}^{B})^{T}
satisfies the following equations:

\begin{split}&(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})f^{\alpha}_{R}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathbf{L}^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}_{R}=\sum_{i=1}^{7}E^{\alpha}_{i}.\end{split}

(3.3)

Here
\mathbf{E}_{i}=(E_{i}^{A},E_{i}^{B})^{T}(i=1,2,3,...,7.)
,

\displaystyle E_{1}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\frac{\nabla_{v}\mu^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}},\quad E_{2}^{\alpha}=-\frac{(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v})\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}f_{R}^{\alpha},

\displaystyle E_{3}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\sum_{\beta=A,B}\varepsilon^{i-1}\Big[\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{i},f^{\beta}_{R})+\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{R},f^{\beta}_{i})\Big],\quad E_{4}^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{k-1}\displaystyle{\sum_{\beta=A,B}\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f_{R}^{\alpha},f_{R}^{\beta})},

\displaystyle E_{5}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{k}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\Big(\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\frac{\nabla_{v}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}f_{R}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{R}^{\alpha}\Big),

\displaystyle E_{6}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\Big[\frac{\nabla_{v}F_{i}^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}+\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}\cdot(\frac{\nabla_{v}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}f_{R}^{\alpha}+\nabla_{v}f_{R}^{\alpha})\Big],

\displaystyle E_{7}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle-\frac{(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}(\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f_{2k-1}^{\alpha})-\mathop{\sum}_{i+j\geq 2k-1\atop 2k-1\geq i,j\geq 1}\frac{\varepsilon^{i+j-6}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{i}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{j}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt+\mathop{\sum}_{i+j\geq 2k\atop 2k-1\geq i,j\geq 1}\sum_{\beta=A,B}\varepsilon^{i+j-6}\Big[\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{i},f^{\beta}_{j})+\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{j},f^{\beta}_{i})\Big].

Then we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta,\phi_{0})
be the solution of the
Euler-Poisson systems (
2.4
) obtained in Lemma

2.1
.
\mathbf{f}_{R}=(f^{A}_{R},f^{B}_{R})^{T}

satisfies (
3.3
). Then, there exist constants

\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, it holds that

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}&\Big(\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\|\sqrt{\theta}f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}+|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{\phi}_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\Big)+\frac{c_{0}}{2\varepsilon}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{\nu}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{R}_{\gamma}\|_{{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}}+\varepsilon^{k-1}\|\mathbf{R}_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}}+\varepsilon^{k}\|\mathbf{R}_{\gamma}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}}|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{\phi}_{R}|_{{L^{2}_{x}}}\\
&\,\,\,+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{q}}(\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}}+|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{\phi}_{R}|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{x}}})+\varepsilon\mathcal{I}_{1}(\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}}+|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{\phi}_{R}|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{x}}})+\varepsilon^{k-1}\mathcal{I}_{2}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{{L^{2}_{x,v}}},\end{split}

(3.4)

where
\mathcal{I}_{1}
,
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are given by the following:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{1}&=\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}[\varepsilon(1+t)]^{i-1}+(\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}[\varepsilon(1+t)]^{i-1})^{2},\\
\mathcal{I}_{2}&=\sum_{2k\leq i+j\leq 4k}\varepsilon^{i+j-2k}(1+t)^{i+j-2}.\end{split}

(3.5)

Proof.

Taking
L^{2}
inner product with
\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}
on both sides of equation (
3.3
), one obtains

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}&\Big[\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\sqrt{\theta}f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})\theta f^{\alpha}_{R},f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\langle L^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}_{R},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big]\\
=\sum_{\alpha=A,B}&\sum_{i=1}^{7}\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{i},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}.\end{split}

(3.6)

In the following, we shall show (
3.4
) for the hard potential case and the soft potential with
-1\leq\gamma\leq 0
, respectively.

First, let us focus on the hard sphere case. To begin with, from the
identity

\frac{e^{\alpha}}{m^{\alpha}}\frac{\nabla_{v}\mu^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}=-e^{\alpha}\frac{v-\mathbf{u}}{\theta}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}
,
we see

\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{1},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}=\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\displaystyle{\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}(v-\mathbf{u})\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f^{\alpha}_{R}\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\,dv\,dx.

Due to

\Delta\phi_{R}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(e^{A}F_{R}^{A}+e^{B}F_{R}^{B}\right)dv

which implies

\begin{split}-\Delta\partial_{t}\phi_{R}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{A}\partial_{t}F_{R}^{A}+e^{B}\partial_{t}F_{R}^{B}dv=\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f^{\alpha}_{R})dv.\end{split}

So, it yields that

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{\phi}_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Delta\partial_{t}\phi_{R}\,\phi_{R}\,dx&=-\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f^{\alpha}_{R})\phi_{R}\,dv\,dx\\
&=\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f^{\alpha}_{R}dv)\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}dx.\end{split}

The second part is bounded by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big|\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{u}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}f^{\alpha}_{R}dv)\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}dx\Big|&=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Delta\partial_{t}\phi_{R}\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}dx\Big|\lesssim|\nabla_{x}\mathbf{u}|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+t)^{q}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}.\end{split}

Next, the term
\frac{1}{2}\left\langle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f^{\alpha}_{R},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}+\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{2},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}
is a cubic polynomial in

v
, which increase as
\{1+|v|\}^{3}
. Let
a_{r}=(3-\gamma)^{-1}
, for any small
constant
\kappa>0
, we devide
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
into
\{|v|<\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}\}
and
\{|v|\geq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}\}
, and notice that

(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}|f_{R}^{\alpha}|\leq(1+|v|)^{l-3}|h^{\alpha}|,\qquad l\geq 7.

It holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\Big(|\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\{\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\}\theta f^{\alpha}_{R},f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}|+|\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{2},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}|\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)|_{W_{x}^{1,\infty}}\|\mathbf{h}(1+|v|^{2})^{\gamma-3}\mathbf{1}_{|v|\geq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(1+|v|^{2})^{-(l+\gamma-3)}dv\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+|(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)|_{W_{x}^{1,\infty}}\,\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathbf{f}_{R}\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{\kappa}\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{R}\,\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}\,\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{\kappa}\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{R}\,\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma}|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}|^{2}\,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{3-\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}dv\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{\kappa}\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{R}\,\mathbf{1}_{|v|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{a_{r}}}}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa^{3-\gamma}}{\varepsilon}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{\nu}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{\kappa}\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{q}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\frac{\kappa^{3-\gamma}}{\varepsilon}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{\nu}^{2}.

For
\gamma=1
(hard sphere), (
2.16
) shows that

\|(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathcal{P}\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}},

where
\frac{1}{(1+t)^{q}}
comes from the decay estimates of
|(n^{A},n^{B},\mathbf{u},\theta)|_{W_{x}^{1,\infty}}
in (
2.8
).

Moreover, using Lemma
3.2
, it is easy to get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\Big|\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{3},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big|=&\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\sum_{\beta=A,B}\varepsilon^{i-1}\left\langle\Big(\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{i},f^{\beta}_{R})+\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f^{\alpha}_{R},f^{\beta}_{i})\Big),\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big|\\
\lesssim&\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}[\varepsilon(1+t)]^{i-1}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{\nu}\\
\lesssim&C_{\kappa}\varepsilon\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathcal{P})\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{\nu}^{2},\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\Big|\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{4},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big|=&\varepsilon^{k-1}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\sum_{\beta=A,B}\Big|\left\langle\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}(f_{R}^{\alpha},f_{R}^{\beta}),\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big|\\
\lesssim&\varepsilon^{k-1}\|\mathbf{h}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x,v}}.\end{split}

Noting the orthogonality relation

\bigl\langle\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{R}^{\alpha},\theta f_{R}^{\alpha}\bigr\rangle_{x,v}=0
,
one obtains

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\Big|\left\langle E^{\alpha}_{5},\theta f^{\alpha}_{R}\right\rangle_{x,v}\Big|=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{k}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}e^{\alpha}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}\cdot\frac{v-\mathbf{u}}{2}(f_{R}^{\alpha})^{2}dvdx

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{k}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v-\mathbf{u}|f^{\alpha}_{R}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{M}^{\alpha}}}{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{l}\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}h^{\alpha}dv|^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{k}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\|(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{|v-\mathbf{u}|^{2}\mu_{M}^{\alpha}}{\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2l}\mu^{\alpha}}dv)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}}\|h^{\alpha}\|_{{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}}\|f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon^{k}\|h^{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\|f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

The decomposition

\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha=A,B}}\bigl\langle E_{6}^{\alpha},\theta f_{R}^{\alpha}\bigr\rangle_{x,v}=:I_{h1}+I_{h2}

leads to

\displaystyle I_{h1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\nabla_{v}F_{i}^{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\alpha}}}\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi^{\alpha}_{R}\theta f_{R}^{\alpha}\,dv\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}\varepsilon^{i}\sum_{\alpha=A,B}\|\theta(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{|\nabla_{v}F_{i}|^{2}}{\mu^{\alpha}}dv)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}}\|f^{\alpha}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\sum_{i=1}^{2k-1}[\varepsilon(1+t)]^{i-1})(|\nabla_{x}\phi_{R}|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{f}_{R}\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}})
